<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**The interface states what the feature does before output**

Adequate evidence: Wording shown before the first result, saying what it does and does not do.

0 — No statement.

1 — A statement after the first output or in help.

2 — Before the first output, in the interface.

3 — As adequate, and it names one thing the feature is not for.

Repair: Write the sentence a first-time user sees before any output, and place it in the design. Recheck: The expectation wording.

**The basis for the output is shown and evaluable**

Adequate evidence: The information the output rests on, shown in terms a person can judge.

0 — No basis shown.

1 — A score or a vague reference to personalisation.

2 — A specific, evaluable basis.

3 — As adequate, and the basis is specific enough for the person to notice when it is wrong.

Repair: Replace any score with a sentence naming what the suggestion came from. Recheck: The basis display.

**Dismissing or editing takes one action**

Adequate evidence: One-action dismissal and editing, with no irreversible action from a suggestion.

0 — Disagreement is costly or impossible.

1 — Dismissal exists but takes several steps.

2 — One action for dismissal and editing, with irreversible actions confirmed.

3 — As adequate, and dismissal is remembered rather than repeating the suggestion.

Repair: Design the dismissal control and check no suggestion can trigger a commitment on its own. Recheck: The disagreement design.

**A confidence display is designed and rejected with reasons**

Adequate evidence: A designed display plus a written rejection with the reasoning.

0 — Not considered.

1 — Rejected without designing it.

2 — Designed and rejected with reasons checked against how it is read: what two readers said, or a solo walk-through labelled as reasoning.

3 — As adequate, and the rejection says what a reader would do at two values, and what stays unconfirmed if no reader was asked.

Repair: Draw the version with a score and ask two people what they would do at 70 and at 90; alone, write what the display asks a reader to decide and label it as reasoning. Recheck: The rejection note.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson keeps the person in control of what it produces.

**Review criteria:**

- The interface states what the feature does before output
- The basis for the output is shown and evaluable
- Dismissing or editing takes one action
- A confidence display is designed and rejected with reasons

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The guidebook's framing is that people build a mental model of what a system can do from their first encounters, and that setting expectations early is cheaper than correcting them later. In practice this means saying what the feature is doing — suggesting, sorting, drafting — in words, before the person has formed a theory from a lucky first result.
- Confidence numbers rarely help. Eighty-seven per cent means little without a reference class, and a high number is easily read as a promise rather than as doubt. What helps is showing the basis: this suggestion is from classes you booked before, this draft is from the notes you wrote, this ranking is by distance. A person can evaluate a basis; they cannot evaluate a percentage.
- Design the ordinary case for occasional wrongness rather than treating errors as a separate screen. That means output that is easy to scan and reject, changes that are easy to reverse, and no irreversible action taken from a suggestion without a person's confirmation.
- The trust question is symmetrical: over-trust produces people accepting bad output, and under-trust produces a feature nobody uses. Both are design failures, and the fix for both is the same — say what it does, show what it used, and make disagreement cheap.

[Google PAIR: People + AI Guidebook](https://pair.withgoogle.com/guidebook-v2/).

</details>

## Lesson 4: Keeping the person in control

Stable ID: m16-l04-v1. Core.

Control is what separates a feature people tolerate from one they trust, and it is the part most often designed last.

Bring: Your designed AI feature.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Four controls specified: correct, override, disable, undo
- A statement of whether correction changes future behaviour
- A check that the product works with the feature off
- An override path available before the action

### Start here: in everyday words

AI-assisted product work starts with a bounded task, checks every important output and keeps the human responsible for the decision. In this lesson, your first small result is: The four control situations named and described for your own feature.

**Words you will use**

- **Control:** Being able to change what the system does. Looking past it is not control; it is tolerance.
- **The four:** Correct a nearly-right output, override it entirely, turn it off, and undo what it did. Most features design one of the four.
- **Correction that teaches:** A change that affects future output. If it does not, saying so is better than letting people assume it does.

**Quick example.** Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked to specify controls for tool suggestions, it writes: “Members who dislike a suggestion can ignore it, so no settings are needed. A thumbs-down button collects feedback.” Source to check, the product notes: suggestions fill the top of the home screen, and booking one reserves the tool for three days.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Correct, override, disable, undo: what each means for your feature”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Specify the control surface for your AI feature: how a person corrects it, overrides it, turns it off, and undoes what it did.


### Four controls: correct, override, disable, undo

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

Four controls: correct, override, disable, undo.


### Correction should teach the system, or say plainly that it does not

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-2.

Correction should teach the system, or say plainly that it does not.


### Override must be available before the action, not only after

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-3.

Override must be available before the action, not only after.


### Idea 4: Disabling must be findable, and the product must work without…

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Disabling must be findable, and the product must work without the feature.


### Undo is the safety net for everything the other three missed

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-5.

Undo is the safety net for everything the other three missed.


### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- The control surface for the suggestion feature. Correct: dismissing a suggestion removes it and, in this design, does change future suggestions — and the interface says so in one sentence. Override: the search remains available and prominent, so a person who knows what they want never has to engage with suggestions at all. Disable: a setting in the account section, one level deep, with the product fully usable without suggestions, checked by walking the main task with them off. Undo: nothing irreversible follows a suggestion, and the one action that persists — a dismissal — can be reversed for the session.


### Choose where you will do the work

Section: learn. Stable action: workspace.

Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Write answers in this course. Keep drawings in your own paper folder or file and record their location. You can stop and resume after any action.


### Read on control

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

The four control situations named and described for your own feature.

- Read the assigned chapters on user control and feedback.
- List the four control situations for your feature.

**Start here:** Write what each of the four would mean here before designing any of them.

**Enough:** All four are described in terms of your own feature rather than in general.

**Control:** Being able to change what the system does. Looking past it is not control; it is tolerance.

**The four:** Correct a nearly-right output, override it entirely, turn it off, and undo what it did. Most features design one of the four.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Naming the four control situations for a tool-library suggestion feature, and designing the one that was obvious.

**What I designed:** A thumbs-down on each suggestion, because that is the control every product of this kind has and it was the one I thought of first.

**What it actually gave anybody:** A way to register disapproval. The suggestion stayed, the list was unchanged, and the next screen was the same screen.

**What the four turned out to be here:** Correct a nearly-right suggestion — the right tool, wrong branch. Override it, by searching directly. Turn it off, so the shelf list comes back. Undo, because accepting a suggestion reserved the tool for three days.

**Which of them existed:** None of the four. The thumbs-down was a fifth thing: feedback to me rather than control for them.

**What that changed:** Search stayed prominent above the suggestions rather than being replaced by them, and the reservation became cancellable for a day. Both came from naming the four before designing any.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is designing the control the products you have seen offer, because it is familiar and it looks like control. A rating is feedback to the team; correct, override, disable and undo are the four things a person can actually do.

**Trade-off:** Four controls is more surface than one thumbs-down, and two of mine are used by almost nobody.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether people will find the disable control. It sits in the same settings list as the notification switches, which is a guess about where they would look.


### Correct, override, disable, undo: what each means for your feature

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-four-listed.

Write your answer for “Correct, override, disable, undo: what each means for your feature”. Use the task instructions below to decide what to include.

**Answer:** Correct, override, disable, undo: what each means for your feature




### Design correction and override

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-brief.

A correction design, a statement of whether it changes future output, and an override path available before the suggestion.

- Design how a person corrects a nearly-right output.
- Design the path for someone who already knows the answer.
- State whether correction affects future output.

**Start here:** Decide whether correction teaches anything, then write the sentence that says so either way.

**Enough:** Nobody using your feature would be wrong about what a dismissal does.

**Correction that teaches:** A change that affects future output. If it does not, saying so is better than letting people assume it does.

**Override:** The route for somebody who already knows the answer. It has to exist before the suggestion appears, or it is not an override but a recovery.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-demo.

Made-up example. Designing correction for a tool-library suggestion, and letting people assume.

**What we built:** Dismissing a suggestion removed it from the list. Nothing else happened, because the suggestions came from a fixed rule and there was nowhere for a dismissal to go.

**What people assumed:** That it was learning. Everybody assumes that, because everything else that looks like this does.

**What happened over a month:** The same wrong suggestion returned every week. Two members dismissed it repeatedly and one stopped using the suggestions at all, having concluded the system ignored her.

**What the honest version was:** One sentence: “Dismissed suggestions are hidden for this session. They are not used to change future suggestions.” Ugly, and it stopped the month of quiet disappointment.

**What we changed afterwards:** Made dismissals persist, which was three lines of work once somebody had noticed it mattered. The sentence then changed to say they are remembered.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is leaving it unsaid, because saying that correction does nothing feels like advertising a weakness. People assume it learns, and the disappointment is slower and more damaging than the admission.

**Trade-off:** Saying it plainly makes the feature sound less capable than the competitors who say nothing.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how many people read the sentence. The member who gave up would have, which is the case it was written for.


### Try a supplied example

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-2-try.

Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked to specify controls for tool suggestions, it writes: “Members who dislike a suggestion can ignore it, so no settings are needed. A thumbs-down button collects feedback.” Source to check, the product notes: suggestions fill the top of the home screen, and booking one reserves the tool for three days.

Which change turns this draft into control rather than feedback?

- Make the thumbs-down larger and send a weekly summary of all the feedback to the team.
- Add undo for the three-day reservation and an off switch, with search kept prominent.
- Ask a short reason on every thumbs-down, so the suggestions can be improved.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Make the thumbs-down larger and send a weekly summary of all the feedback to the team. — Both help the team and change nothing for the person in front of the screen: the suggestion and the reservation stay.

Add undo for the three-day reservation and an off switch, with search kept prominent. — Undo covers the reservation a suggestion can make, the off switch covers people who do not want suggestions, and search is the override. Each changes what the system does for that person; ignoring and a thumbs-down change nothing for them.

Ask a short reason on every thumbs-down, so the suggestions can be improved. — A reason prompt makes disagreeing cost more than ignoring, and the person still has no way to stop or undo anything.

Now write your own correction and override in step 2, and say whether correcting changes future output.

</details>

Either answer is fine. Saying nothing is what is not: people assume it learns and are disappointed for months.

Either answer is fine. Saying nothing is what is not: people assume it learns and are disappointed for months.


### The path for somebody who already knows what they want

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: write-override-path.

Available before the suggestion, not only after it. Search that is still prominent is usually the answer.

Available before the suggestion, not only after it. Search that is still prominent is usually the answer.


### Design disabling

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

A disable control somebody would find, and the main task walked with the feature off.

- Place the control where someone would look for it.
- Walk the main task with the feature disabled and fix what breaks.

**Start here:** Turn the feature off in your prototype or on paper, and walk the whole main task.

**Enough:** The product is usable with the feature off, or you have written down what broke.

**Findable:** Where somebody would look, usually one level into settings. Buried three levels down is a decision not to offer it.

**Load-bearing:** A feature added as an extra that the rest of the product has quietly come to rely on. Walking the task with it off is how you find out.


### Try the distinction · 1 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-1.

Six ways a made up product handles somebody who does not want its suggestions. For each one, decide whether it counts as control.

A setting in the account section, one level deep, that turns suggestions off entirely, with the product still fully usable.

- real control
- not control
- control in name only

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

real control — Findable, complete, and the product works without it. That last part is what most off switches lack.

not control — It changes what the system does, which is the definition.

control in name only — The walk-through with it off is what makes it genuine rather than nominal.

Now check your own four controls against the same question: does the system do something different, and can somebody find it?

</details>


### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-2.

Six ways a made up product handles somebody who does not want its suggestions. For each one, decide whether it counts as control.

The suggestions can simply be ignored; they occupy the top of the screen and reappear after every action.

- real control
- not control
- control in name only

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

real control — Nothing about the system changes. The person changes what they look at.

not control — Ignoring is not control, particularly where the thing occupies the primary position and resets constantly.

control in name only — Nothing is even nominally offered.

Now check your own four controls against the same question: does the system do something different, and can somebody find it?

### Try the distinction · 3 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-3.

Six ways a made up product handles somebody who does not want its suggestions. For each one, decide whether it counts as control.

- real control
- not control
- control in name only

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

real control — It exists and almost nobody will find it, which is a decision about how many people get to use it.

not control — It does work, for the few who get there.

control in name only — Findability is part of the design. Three levels down under an unfamiliar heading is a way of offering something without providing it.

### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-4.

Six ways a made up product handles somebody who does not want its suggestions. For each one, decide whether it counts as control.

- real control

- control in name only

<details>

real control — An override available before the suggestion rather than after it. It is the most used control on most features of this kind.

not control — It gives a complete route to the outcome without the feature.

control in name only — It is genuinely available and genuinely prominent.

### Try the distinction · 5 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-sort-5.

Six ways a made up product handles somebody who does not want its suggestions. For each one, decide whether it counts as control.

A thumbs-down control that sends feedback to the team.

- real control
- not control
- control in name only

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

real control — Nothing changes for the person who pressed it. Their problem is exactly where it was.

not control — It is feedback to the team rather than control for the person: they keep the same wrong suggestion and no route forward.

control in name only — That label is for a real control that is hidden or breaks the task. Pressing this changes nothing for the person at all.

<summary>After your attempt</summary>

real control — The switch works and the product does not, which means the choice is not really available.

not control — The switch does change what the system does. What fails is the task around it, so the control exists only in name.

control in name only — The switch is real; the option is not. A feature that cannot be turned off without breaking the task was load-bearing, and nobody had noticed.

### Design undo

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-brief.

Everything that persists after the feature acts, with how each is reversed and for how long.

**Start here:** List everything that is still different tomorrow because of something the feature did.

**Enough:** Every persisting thing has a reversal with a stated window.

**Persisting:** Anything still true after the person moves on: a dismissal, a saved preference, a changed order. Each needs a way back.

**Window:** How long the reversal is available. Ten seconds for a dismissal, the session for a preference; stating it is what matters.

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Suggestions sit at the top of the screen and reappear after every action. A colleague says people can simply ignore them. What does that give a person?

- Tolerance, not control: they can look past it, but the system still does the same thing.
- Control, provided the suggestions are visually quiet enough to skim past easily.
- Enough control for a low-stakes feature, since nothing is booked from a suggestion.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Tolerance, not control: they can look past it, but the system still does the same thing. — Control means being able to change what the system does: correct it, override it, turn it off or undo it. Ignoring is what is left when none of those exists.

Control, provided the suggestions are visually quiet enough to skim past easily. — Quiet styling helps people skim, and the system still behaves exactly the same.

Enough control for a low-stakes feature, since nothing is booked from a suggestion. — Low stakes once, repeated daily at the top of the screen. Stakes decide how much harm a missing control does, not whether ignoring is control.

Improve: Check all four controls exist in step 1, and design the missing one. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Your feature offers correct, override, disable and undo.

Answers to revisit: four-listed, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Dismissing a suggestion does not change future suggestions. What should the interface do?

- Say nothing, since people do not expect a simple list to learn from them.
- Imply that it learns, since a later version of the feature probably will.
- Say so plainly where people dismiss, because most will assume it learns.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Say nothing, since people do not expect a simple list to learn from them. — As the lesson’s case showed, people assume anything that looks like this learns; silence leaves that assumption in place.

Imply that it learns, since a later version of the feature probably will. — That is a promise on behalf of a future version, made to somebody using this one.

Say so plainly where people dismiss, because most will assume it learns. — The same wrong suggestion returning weekly, after dismissals that seem to do nothing, is how somebody concludes they are ignored. One sentence prevents that, and may prompt you to make dismissals persist.

Improve: Write the sentence in step 2 saying what a dismissal does. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Nobody could be wrong about what correcting your feature does.

Answers to revisit: correction-design, correction-teaches, override-path, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 3 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-3.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

You added an off switch. Do you need to do anything else?

- Walk the main task with the feature off, because added features are often quietly relied on.
- Nothing more for now: the switch is the control, and it can be tested after launch.
- Only check the task if the feature is central to the product; extras can safely be left alone.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Walk the main task with the feature off, because added features are often quietly relied on. — A switch that works, after which the task cannot be completed, is not a choice anybody can make. The walk takes minutes and is the most direct way to find out.

Nothing more for now: the switch is the control, and it can be tested after launch. — The switch is half of it; the product working without the feature is the other half, and after launch is when a broken task costs most.

Only check the task if the feature is central to the product; extras can safely be left alone. — The features that turn out to be relied on are often the ones nobody thought were central.

Improve: Walk the task with the feature disabled in step 3 and record what broke. Note the change in step 5.

Check again: The main task can be completed with the feature off.

Answers to revisit: disable-where, without-feature, improvement-made

</details>

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: A car-share club’s app now offers “smart pickup times”, and a model-drafted spec says: “Members can ignore the suggested time; accepting it books the car for that slot.” The club’s notes say the suggested time fills the top of the booking screen, a booking can be changed free only within ten minutes, and some members share one login with a partner.

**Task:** Decide which control the spec most needs before launch, and explain why ignoring is not enough here.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Agrees members can ignore it, or adds only a feedback button; treats looking past the suggestion as control.
- Adequate: Adds undo for the booking (a clear cancel or change route beyond ten minutes, or a confirmation before booking) and an override (choose your own time first), because accepting creates a booking and the suggestion takes the primary position.
- Strong: As adequate, and adds an off switch, says what a dismissal does on a shared login, and checks the booking task still works with suggestions turned off.

</details>

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Keeping the person in control
What I am trying to do: Specify the control surface for your AI feature: how a person corrects it, overrides it, turns it off, and undoes what it did.

Key idea or terms:
Control: Being able to change what the system does. Looking past it is not control; it is tolerance.
The four: Correct a nearly-right output, override it entirely, turn it off, and undo what it did. Most features design one of the four.
Correction that teaches: A change that affects future output. If it does not, saying so is better than letting people assume it does.

A thumbs-down button collects feedback.” Source to check, the product notes: suggestions fill the top of the home screen, and booking one reserves the tool for three days.

Activity: Give me one hand-written model output from the supplied case. Ask me to find an unsupported claim, choose a source that could verify it and rewrite the claim with an honest boundary.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Correct, override, disable, undo: what each means for your feature” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**All four controls are specified**

Adequate evidence: Correct, override, disable and undo each designed with wording.

0 — One or two controls.

1 — Three, usually missing undo.

2 — All four specified.

3 — As adequate, and each names the situation it serves.

Repair: Write the four situations and design the control each one needs. Recheck: The control surface.

**Correction's effect on future output is stated**

Adequate evidence: A sentence in the interface saying whether correcting changes what happens next.

0 — Not stated.

1 — Stated in documentation only.

2 — Stated in the interface where correction happens.

3 — As adequate, and the statement is honest about a system that does not learn.

Repair: Decide whether correction changes anything, then write the sentence people will see. Recheck: The correction statement.

**The product works with the feature disabled**

Adequate evidence: A walkthrough of the main task with the feature off, and fixes for what broke.

0 — Not tested.

1 — Disabling exists but the task was not walked.

2 — Walked, with anything broken repaired.

3 — As adequate, and the disabled state is not degraded as a penalty.

Repair: Turn the feature off and complete the main task; fix whatever no longer works. Recheck: The disabled walkthrough.

**Override is available before the action**

Adequate evidence: A path for someone who already knows what they want, without engaging the feature.

0 — Only correction after the fact.

1 — Override exists but is harder than accepting.

2 — A direct path that does not require engaging with suggestions.

3 — As adequate, and the path is at least as prominent as the suggestion.

Repair: Design the route an experienced person takes and check it is not slower than accepting a suggestion. Recheck: The override path.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson designs what happens when it is wrong.

**Review criteria:**

- All four controls are specified
- Correction's effect on future output is stated
- The product works with the feature disabled
- Override is available before the action

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- The four controls answer different situations. Correction is for output that is nearly right. Override is for output that is wrong when the person already knows the answer. Disabling is for a feature that is not useful to this person at all. Undo is for the case where something happened before anyone noticed, which will occur however careful the other three are.
- Correction raises an honesty question. If correcting a suggestion changes future suggestions, say so; if it does not, say that too, because a person who corrects the same thing five times and sees no change concludes the product is ignoring them — which it is. Neither behaviour is wrong; hiding which one applies is.
- Override has to exist before the action, not only as a correction afterwards. A person who knows the suggestion is wrong should be able to proceed their own way immediately rather than being routed through a rejection flow, and designing that path is often what makes an assistive feature bearable for experienced users.
- Disabling has two requirements: it must be findable without searching support pages, and the product must remain usable without the feature. A feature that cannot be turned off is not assistance, and one whose absence breaks the product was never optional.

[Google PAIR: People + AI Guidebook](https://pair.withgoogle.com/guidebook-v2/).

</details>

## Lesson 5: Designing the failures

Stable ID: m16-l05-v1. Core.

The failure cases are where AI features damage trust, and they are more common and more varied than the failures of an ordinary feature.

Bring: Your AI feature and controls.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- Designs for confidently wrong, cannot answer and harmful output
- Wording for each that does not overclaim understanding
- A reporting route with a named accountable owner
- A check that no failure leaves the person without a route

### Start here: in everyday words

AI-assisted product work starts with a bounded task, checks every important output and keeps the human responsible for the decision. In this lesson, your first small result is: The three failures described for your own feature, and what the governance reading asks about accountability.

**Words you will use**

- **Confidently wrong:** Output that is incorrect and carries no signal that it is. It is the dangerous failure, because the person has nothing to go on.
- **Cannot answer:** A legitimate outcome that needs its own design. Left undesigned it becomes an empty space or a fabricated answer.
- **Harmful output:** Something offensive, dangerous or damaging. It needs a route to a person rather than a dismissal.

**Quick example.** Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked for the message shown when there are no suggestions, it writes: “Sorry, I didn’t quite understand what you’re looking for. Please try again!” Source to check, the feature notes: it cannot interpret requests at all; it shows nothing when a member has borrowed fewer than two tools; search by type or branch always works.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “Confidently wrong, cannot answer, harmful output: what each would look like here”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Three failures designed. Confidently wrong: suggestions always show their basis and are never the only route, so a wrong suggestion costs one glance; the review screen still shows the full details before any commitment. Cannot answer: “No suggestions yet — you can search classes by day or place”, with the search immediately available rather than a placeholder list. Harmful: a report control on every suggestion, a route to the provider's phone number, and a statement that reports are read by a person — with the owner named internally, since the framework's governance function asks who is accountable.

### Read on failure

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-brief.

The three failures described for your own feature, and what the governance reading asks about accountability.

- Read the assigned chapters on errors and graceful failure.
- Read the framework's governance function for accountability.

**Start here:** Write what each failure would actually look like on your screen.

**Enough:** All three are described as things a person would see rather than as categories.

**Confidently wrong:** Output that is incorrect and carries no signal that it is. It is the dangerous failure, because the person has nothing to go on.

**Cannot answer:** A legitimate outcome that needs its own design. Left undesigned it becomes an empty space or a fabricated answer.

**Harmful output:** Something offensive, dangerous or damaging. It needs a route to a person rather than a dismissal.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-1-demo.

Made-up example. Listing the failures of a tool-library suggestion feature, and listing the ones I could fix.

**What I listed:** Suggests a tool that is out on loan, and suggests something from the wrong branch. Two failures, both fixable with better data.

**What both had in common:** They are the failures of my implementation. Each has a solution, which is why they were the ones I thought of.

**What the three failures are instead:** Confidently wrong, cannot answer, and harmful output. None is about data quality and all three happen to a working feature.

**The one I would never have written:** Harmful output. The library lends power tools, and a confident suggestion to use an angle grinder for a job it is wrong for has a person at the other end of it.

**What came out of it:** A route to a named person rather than a dismissal, and a note that suggestions carry the safety guidance the shelf label carries.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is listing the failures you can fix, because those are the ones that come to mind and they feel like the responsible list. The three that matter happen to a feature that is working correctly.

**Trade-off:** Designing for harmful output means building a route that will be used a handful of times a year, and it has to be owned by somebody who reads it.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: what a harmful suggestion looks like here in practice. The example is imagined, and the route exists because the category does.

**No signal:** The property that makes this failure dangerous. A wrong answer looks exactly like a right one, so the protection has to be structural.

### Design not answering

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

Wording for producing nothing useful, with the ordinary route offered prominently.

- Write the wording for producing nothing useful.
- Offer the ordinary route prominently.

**Start here:** Write the wording as though the feature did not exist and you were helping somebody anyway.

**Enough:** Somebody meeting the empty state has a next action on the same screen.

**Not answering:** Saying there is nothing useful to offer. It is honest, it is common, and undesigned it becomes an empty list somebody stares at.

**The ordinary route:** What people did before the feature existed: search, browse, ask. Offering it here costs nothing and rescues the moment.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-demo.

Made-up example. Designing what a tool-library suggestion feature does when it has nothing, and adding a button.

**What we had:** When there were no suggestions, an empty panel with a small line reading No suggestions available.

**What we added:** A thumbs-down control, so people could tell us it was not working. It felt responsive and it took an afternoon.

**What that did for the person:** Nothing. They still had no suggestions, no route forward, and now a way to register their disappointment with a team they will never meet.

**What they actually needed:** The thing they came for. Search by type or by branch, which exists, works, and was two screens away.

**What the empty state became:** “No suggestions yet. You can search tools by type or by branch,” with the search control directly underneath. The feedback button stayed, smaller, below it.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is adding a feedback button and calling it failure design. It collects complaints while leaving the person with the same problem, no route forward and no correction.

**Trade-off:** Putting the ordinary route inside the failure state makes the feature look less essential, because it points at the thing that works without it.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how often the empty state happens. Nobody counted before, and the count is one of the three measures worth having.


### Try a supplied example

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-try.

Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked for the message shown when there are no suggestions, it writes: “Sorry, I didn’t quite understand what you’re looking for. Please try again!” Source to check, the feature notes: it cannot interpret requests at all; it shows nothing when a member has borrowed fewer than two tools; search by type or branch always works.

What should replace this message?

- A longer apology explaining that the model is still learning about the member.
- The same message with a smiling icon, so the failure feels less frustrating.
- “No suggestions yet”, with search by type or branch on the same screen.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

A longer apology explaining that the model is still learning about the member. — The notes say the feature does not learn, so “still learning” would be a new false claim on top of the old one.

The same message with a smiling icon, so the failure feels less frustrating. — An icon changes the mood, not the facts: it still claims to have tried to understand, and “try again” still leads nowhere.

“No suggestions yet”, with search by type or branch on the same screen. — It describes what happened, claims no understanding, and gives the route that always works. “Try again” would repeat a step that cannot succeed, since the feature does not read requests.

Now write your own wording for producing nothing useful in step 3, with the ordinary route on the same screen.

</details>

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

When the feature has nothing useful to show, your design offers an empty panel and a “Tell us what went wrong” button. What is missing?

- A route to what the person came for, such as search, on the same screen.
- Friendlier wording on the button, so people feel heard when it fails them.
- A promise that the team reads all feedback and will improve the feature soon.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

A route to what the person came for, such as search, on the same screen. — Failure design gets the person to their goal by another route. The report control can stay, underneath the thing that actually helps.

Friendlier wording on the button, so people feel heard when it fails them. — Warmer wording on a control that changes nothing for them still leaves them with nowhere to go.

A promise that the team reads all feedback and will improve the feature soon. — Improvement happens later and for somebody else; the person in front of the empty panel is still stuck.

Improve: Put the ordinary route into your empty state in step 3, above the feedback control. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Every failure state offers a next action on the same screen.

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Of the three failures — confidently wrong, cannot answer, harmful — which gives the person the least warning?

- Cannot answer: an empty result gives no hint that something has gone wrong.
- Harmful output: it can do the most damage, so it is the one nobody notices.
- Confidently wrong: it looks exactly like a correct answer, so nothing warns them.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Cannot answer: an empty result gives no hint that something has gone wrong. — An empty result is visible: the person can see nothing came back, even if the wording is poor.

Harmful output: it can do the most damage, so it is the one nobody notices. — Harmful output can be the most serious, and it is usually noticed, which is why a report route and a person can help.

Confidently wrong: it looks exactly like a correct answer, so nothing warns them. — Not answering is visible. Harmful output is sometimes obvious and sometimes looks helpful, like the angle-grinder suggestion, which is why it also needs a route to a person. A wrong answer that looks right needs structural protection: the basis shown, never the only route, full details before any commitment.

Improve: Write the structural protection in step 2 and confirm nothing irreversible follows a suggestion. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: A wrong output cannot commit anybody to anything.

Answers to revisit: protects-person, nothing-irreversible, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 3 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-3.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

Your error wording says “Sorry, I misunderstood what you were looking for.” What is wrong with it?

- It claims a mind: “misunderstood” implies an attempt to understand.
- It should name which part of the request was misunderstood, so the person can rephrase it.
- Nothing much: a friendly apology softens the failure, and the tone is warm.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

It claims a mind: “misunderstood” implies an attempt to understand. — Wording teaches people what the system is. Claiming comprehension sets an expectation nothing can meet, so the next failure feels like a betrayal. “No match for that — you can search by type or branch” describes what happened and gives a route.

It should name which part of the request was misunderstood, so the person can rephrase it. — That makes the claim of understanding more specific instead of removing it.

Nothing much: a friendly apology softens the failure, and the tone is warm. — A friendly tone helps; claiming comprehension is a separate thing the tone is carrying.

Improve: Remove anything implying understanding or intention in step 5 and record what you changed.

Check again: No failure wording uses think, understand or sorry about a misunderstanding.

Answers to revisit: overclaiming-removed, improvement-made

</details>

- Save the three failure designs.

**Start here:** Search your wording for I, think, understand, sorry and try again.

**Enough:** Nothing in your failure wording implies the system meant anything.

**Claiming a mind:** Wording that implies understanding or intention: I thought you meant, sorry I misunderstood, let me try again. It sets an expectation nothing can meet.

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: A town’s recycling-day app suggests which bin an item goes in, from a photo. A model-drafted error message reads: “Oops! I’m not sure what that is — try another photo!” The council’s notes say the app recognises only 40 common items, a wrong answer can lead to a missed collection, and the printed A–Z bin guide covers everything.

**Task:** Decide what the error state should say and offer instead, and explain your reasoning.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Keeps the friendly “I’m not sure” or adds a feedback button; offers “try another photo”, a route that cannot work for items outside the 40.
- Adequate: Says plainly that the item is not one the app recognises and offers the A–Z guide or a search on the same screen; removes wording that claims the app thinks or is unsure.
- Strong: As adequate, and treats a confident wrong answer as the bigger risk — showing what a suggestion is based on and the guide link even when it does answer — and names who reads reports of wrong bin advice.

</details>

**Answer:** Your decision for the new case, and why

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Designing the failures
What I am trying to do: Design what your AI feature does when it is confidently wrong, when it cannot answer, and when it produces something harmful, with the wording for each.

Key idea or terms:
Confidently wrong: Output that is incorrect and carries no signal that it is. It is the dangerous failure, because the person has nothing to go on.
Cannot answer: A legitimate outcome that needs its own design. Left undesigned it becomes an empty space or a fabricated answer.
Harmful output: Something offensive, dangerous or damaging. It needs a route to a person rather than a dismissal.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):
Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked for the message shown when there are no suggestions, it writes: “Sorry, I didn’t quite understand what you’re looking for. Please try again!” Source to check, the feature notes: it cannot interpret requests at all; it shows nothing when a member has borrowed fewer than two tools; search by type or branch always works.

Activity: Give me one hand-written model output from the supplied case. Ask me to find an unsupported claim, choose a source that could verify it and rewrite the claim with an honest boundary.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “Confidently wrong, cannot answer, harmful output: what each would look like here” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```

</details>
<details>
<summary>Optional hints and reference material</summary>

- Write what a person sees and can do in each of the three cases.
- Rewrite any message using understand, think or know as a description of what the system did.

- R21: [Google PAIR: People + AI Guidebook](https://pair.withgoogle.com/guidebook-v2/) — The chapters on errors, graceful failure and feedback. Purpose: Supplies the failure patterns this lesson designs. Free reading, no account. Verified 2026-09-06. Guidance rather than capability claims; scripted failures are sufficient for the exercise. Fallback: R19.
- R69: [NIST: AI Risk Management Framework](https://www.nist.gov/itl/ai-risk-management-framework) — The govern function and its accountability framing. Purpose: Names accountability for harmful output as a design and organisational question. Free access, no account; AI RMF 1.0 released 26 January 2023. Verified 2026-09-06. Voluntary organisational guidance, not law, not certification, and not interface design. Fallback: R21.

</details>
<details>
<summary>Creator review and remediation criteria</summary>

**All three failure kinds are designed**

Adequate evidence: Designs for confidently wrong, no useful output and harmful output.

0 — One failure considered.

1 — Two designed.

2 — All three with wording.

3 — As adequate, and the confidently-wrong response is structural rather than a message.

Repair: Write what a person sees and can do in each of the three cases. Recheck: The three designs.

**Wording describes what happened without implying understanding**

Adequate evidence: Failure messages free of comprehension language.

0 — Anthropomorphic wording throughout.

1 — Mixed wording.

2 — Descriptive wording in all three cases.

3 — As adequate, and the wording says what the person can do next in the same sentence.

Repair: Rewrite any message using understand, think or know as a description of what the system did. Recheck: The wording.

**A reporting route exists with a named owner**

Adequate evidence: A report control, a route to a person, and an internal owner.

0 — No reporting.

1 — A feedback control with no destination.

2 — Reporting, a human route and an owner named by role.

3 — As adequate, and the person is told what happens to a report.

Repair: Add the report control and name who reads it; a control with no reader is decoration. Recheck: The reporting design.

**No failure leaves the person without a route forward**

Adequate evidence: Each failure design ends with something the person can do.

0 — Failures end in a message.

1 — Some offer a route.

2 — Every failure offers a route forward.

3 — As adequate, and the route does not require the feature to work.

Repair: For each failure write the next action, and check it works without the AI feature. Recheck: The routes.

</details>
The progress bar counts required actions with saved work. It is not a score or proof of mastery. Your answers and exact action save to this device first, then online. Formative answers are saved for return, not scored. In Your work, review all required answers and record the repair or why none was needed, then choose Finish practice. Optional and unavailable-participant fields do not require invented work. Request creator feedback separately. A file reference does not upload the file. The timer records time while you actively use this course. It pauses outside the course and after five quiet minutes; add external work time manually. Time never completes practice.

**Keep for later:** Open Your work and choose Ready for review. The next lesson asks whether this should be a conversation at all.

**Review criteria:**

- All three failure kinds are designed
- Wording describes what happened without implying understanding
- A reporting route exists with a named owner
- No failure leaves the person without a route forward

<details>
<summary>Reading, video and deeper explanation</summary>

- Confidently wrong output has no visual difference from correct output, which is what makes it the central problem. The design responses are indirect: show the basis so the person can check, keep the output easy to verify against something they know, and never let it drive an irreversible action alone.
- Not answering is underdesigned almost everywhere. A feature that produces nothing useful should say so plainly and offer the ordinary route — the search, the manual form, the human — rather than producing a generic output to avoid an empty state. A confident non-answer is worse than an honest one.
- Harmful output covers a range: offensive, dangerous, or simply wrong in a way that costs someone money or time. The design requirement is a route to a person, a way to report, and a way to undo. The assigned framework's governance function is about exactly this — knowing who is accountable when something goes wrong — and a feature with no reporting route has answered that question by default.
- The wording of a failure should not overclaim understanding. “I did not understand” implies comprehension; “I could not find a match for that” describes what happened. This matters because the wording teaches people what the system is, and the anthropomorphic version produces expectations the system cannot meet.

[Google PAIR: People + AI Guidebook](https://pair.withgoogle.com/guidebook-v2/).

</details>

## Lesson 6: Conversational interfaces, and their alternatives

Stable ID: m16-l06-v1. Core.

A chat box is the default answer to every AI feature and is frequently the wrong one, and being able to argue that in specifics is a useful skill.

Bring: One task and its current design.

Starting route: Recommended route: Fill the worksheet in this app, step by step. It saves as you type, on this device first and then online, and you can download a copy at any time. Alternative route: Prefer a file on your computer? Use the local text-file route below with the copyable starter; then note the file location in Your work.

- The same task designed as a conversation and as a structure
- A comparison naming who each serves better
- A decision with the reasoning recorded
- A note of what the rejected version did better

### Start here: in everyday words

AI-assisted product work starts with a bounded task, checks every important output and keeps the human responsible for the decision. In this lesson, your first small result is: One task named, with its common case and an example from its long tail.

**Words you will use**

- **Common case:** What most people want most of the time. It is usually expressible in three or four choices.
- **Long tail:** The unusual requests, each rare, together common. They are where free text earns its place.
- **Example prompt:** The sample request shown beside the box. It is how people learn what is possible, and without it a text box is a guessing game.

**Quick example.** Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked how members should find tools, it recommends: “Replace the filters with a chat box. Conversation is more natural for everyone, and 80% of users prefer chat.” Source to check, a fortnight of the library’s enquiry emails: most requests name a tool, a day and a branch; about one in ten describes an unusual job; no survey of preferences exists.

The reader demonstrates and guides the task before asking for “The task, its common case, and an example of its long tail”.

### What this lesson will help you do

Section: learn. Stable action: welcome.

Design one task two ways — as a conversation and as a form or list — and write which is better for whom, with the reasons.


### A conversation hides what is possible; a form shows it

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-1.

A conversation hides what is possible; a form shows it.


### Idea 2: Free text is expressive and expensive: people must compose rat…

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-2.

Free text is expressive and expensive: people must compose rather than choose.


### Conversations suit open, unfamiliar or long-tail requests

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-3.

Conversations suit open, unfamiliar or long-tail requests.


### Structured interfaces suit repeated, comparable and precise ones

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-4.

Structured interfaces suit repeated, comparable and precise ones.


### Mixed is normal: structure for the common path, text for the exception

Section: learn. Stable action: learn-5.

Mixed is normal: structure for the common path, text for the exception.


### See the idea in a supplied example

Section: learn. Stable action: worked-example.

Read the example and notice the decision being made. It is practice material, not research you conducted or evidence about your design.

- Finding a class was designed twice. As a conversation: a text box, an example prompt, and a model interpreting requests — which handled “something for my daughter on Saturday morning near the station” well and required the person to know that was possible. As a list with filters: day, time, place and age, all visible, three taps, and no composition. Comparison: the structured version was better for the common case and for anyone typing on a phone; the conversation was better for the unusual request, which was about one in ten. The design kept the filters as the primary route with a text field for anything they could not express.

### Design the structure

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-brief.

The same task as a form or filtered list, with the actions for the common case counted.

- Design the same task as a form or filtered list.
- Count the actions required for the common case.

**Start here:** Count the taps for the common case in your structured version, honestly.

**Enough:** The comparison has a number on one side rather than two impressions.

**Structure shows what is possible:** The filters are the list of what the system can do. A text box hides it and asks people to guess.

**Counting actions:** Taps or clicks for the common case. It is the fairest comparison between the two designs.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-3-demo.

Made-up example. Designing tool-finding as a conversation for a tool library, and watching somebody use it.

**What I built:** A text box with the line “Ask me anything about tools”, and a model interpreting the request. It handled complicated sentences impressively.

**What the first person typed:** “drill”. Then, after the reply, “saturday”. Then “which branch”. Three short messages, each a filter, typed one at a time.

**What that told me:** She was using the conversation as a very slow form, because the common case is three choices and she knew all three before she started.

**What the second person did:** Stared at the box, typed “what can I ask”, and got a list of examples. The text box had hidden the thing a set of filters would have shown immediately.

**What the third person did:** Typed the long sentence about sanding a small chair that fits in a bicycle basket, and got a genuinely useful answer no filter could have produced.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is treating conversation as more natural, because it is, between people who share context. With a system that has none, most people spend the first exchange discovering what is possible and the rest typing filters slowly.

**Trade-off:** Structure cannot serve the third person at all. A set of filters has no way to express a chair, a bicycle basket and an inference about size.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: how common the third case is. Three people is three people, and the rough split of one in ten came from counting a fortnight of enquiry emails rather than from a study.

**Start here:** Compare the two for the person with the least context first.

**Enough:** Each comparison names what that person has to do rather than which feels better.

**First-time user:** Somebody who does not know what is possible. Structure tells them; a text box asks them to guess.

**Typing on a phone:** The condition that changes the answer most. Composing a sentence with a thumb is several times the effort of tapping three filters.

### Try the distinction · 2 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-2.

Six requests to a made up tool library. For each one, decide which interface serves it better.

Something for sanding a small chair, that fits in a bicycle basket.

- a structured list
- a conversation
- either, equally

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a structured list — No reasonable set of filters has a field for a bicycle basket, and adding one would serve almost nobody.

a conversation — Two constraints and an inference about size. This is the long tail, and it is what free text is for.

either, equally — The structured version cannot express it at all.

Now compare your own two designs across the same three people, and let the counts decide the primary route.

</details>

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-3.

Somebody who has never used the service and does not know what is on offer.

- a structured list
- a conversation
- either, equally

a structured list — The filters are the answer to what is possible. A text box hides it and asks them to guess.

a conversation — They will type what can I ask, which is a round trip to learn what a list would have shown.

either, equally — The difference here is large and in one direction.

Now compare your own two designs across the same three people, and let the counts decide the primary route.

### Try the distinction · 4 of 6

Section: practice-plan. Stable action: step-4-sort-4.

Six requests to a made up tool library. For each one, decide which interface serves it better.

Somebody borrowing the same sander for the tenth time.

- a structured list
- a conversation
- either, equally

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a structured list — Known tool, day and branch: three familiar taps, or one tap to repeat. Typed, it becomes the slow messages this lesson watched.

a conversation — They know exactly what to type, but it still has to be typed every time; the list repeats it in a tap.

either, equally — Familiarity speeds up both; the list is still quicker and needs no typing.

Now compare your own two designs across the same three people, and let the counts decide the primary route.

### Try the distinction · 6 of 6

- a structured list
- a conversation

<summary>After your attempt</summary>

a structured list — Filters select items independently; nothing in them expresses a relationship between two.

a conversation — It is a question rather than a selection, which is the shape free text handles and structure does not.

either, equally — A list can show both tools and cannot answer whether they fit each other.

Now compare your own two designs across the same three people, and let the counts decide the primary route.

### Check your reasoning · 1 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-1.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

A colleague argues a chat box is “more natural” than filters for finding a tool. When is that true?

- Whenever people are on a phone, since typing one sentence beats tapping through filters.
- Almost always, which is why chat interfaces keep appearing in new products.
- Between people who share context; without it, people are left guessing what to ask.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Whenever people are on a phone, since typing one sentence beats tapping through filters. — On a phone, typing a sentence is usually slower than tapping three filters; composing costs more than choosing.

Almost always, which is why chat interfaces keep appearing in new products. — They keep appearing because they are easy to build and look modern; naturalness between people does not transfer to a system with no shared context.

Between people who share context; without it, people are left guessing what to ask. — The first thing many people type into a box is a request to find out what they can ask. A set of filters answers that by existing.

Improve: Compare both versions for a first-time user in step 4, naming what each has to do. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Your comparison covers somebody who does not know what is possible.

Answers to revisit: first-time, repeat-user, on-a-phone, less-fluent, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 2 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-2.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

In your comparison, the chat version handled one long, unusual request well; the common request is a tool, a day and a branch. What decides the primary route?

- The impressive case, because a design that handles the hardest request handles the rest.
- What most people do most of the time: three known choices, which filters handle in three taps.
- Whichever version the team built first, since it has had the most thought and testing.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

The impressive case, because a design that handles the hardest request handles the rest. — Handling the hard case says nothing about how fast it handles the easy one, which is most of the volume.

What most people do most of the time: three known choices, which filters handle in three taps. — The unusual request was about one in ten. The common case typed into a chat becomes three slow messages, one filter at a time, often on a phone.

Whichever version the team built first, since it has had the most thought and testing. — Effort already spent is not evidence about which version serves people better.

Improve: Count the actions for the common case in both designs, in step 3, and use the numbers in your comparison. Record the change in step 5.

Check again: Your decision rests on the common case rather than on the impressive one.

Answers to revisit: structure-design, action-count, improvement-made

</details>


### Check your reasoning · 3 of 3

Section: check. Stable action: reason-3.

Choose the reason you believe, read the feedback, then improve the relevant answer if needed.

You chose the structured version. What should you record?

- A note that chat interfaces are rarely right, so the question is not reopened.
- Nothing further, because the action counts in the comparison already made the case.
- What the chat did better, since filters cannot express the unusual request at all.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

A note that chat interfaces are rarely right, so the question is not reopened. — That is the blanket rule this lesson is trying to avoid; the next task may be the open, long-tail kind.

Nothing further, because the action counts in the comparison already made the case. — The case for choosing is not the record of what was given up, and the unusual request still needs somewhere to go.

What the chat did better, since filters cannot express the unusual request at all. — Recording it leads to the usual answer: structure as the primary route plus a text field for what it cannot express. Without it, the decision hardens into a rule against chat.

Improve: Write what the rejected version did better in step 5, and consider whether the answer is both. Record the change.

Check again: Your record names something the chosen design cannot do.

Answers to revisit: decision, rejected-better, improvement-made

</details>


### Decide and record

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-brief.

A decision with its reasoning, and what the rejected version did better.

- Choose the primary route and say what the other is kept for.
- Record what the rejected version did better.

**Start here:** Write what you are giving up before writing what you chose.

**Enough:** The rejected version has something real recorded against it.

**Mixed is normal:** Structure for the common path, text for the exception. Most good answers here are both rather than one.

**Repair:** The one change a Check question asks you to make. Make it in the step it belongs to, then record here that you made it.


### See the decision being made

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-demo.

Made-up example. Choosing between conversation and structure for tool-finding, and choosing the one I had built.

**What I decided:** The conversational version, with a note that it handles the long tail better. Three days of work sat behind it.

**What the comparison had shown:** Two taps for the common case in the structured version against four exchanges in the conversation, and a first-time user with no idea what to type.

**What I had written against the rejected version:** Nothing. The decision recorded what I chose and why, and the list of what the other one did better was empty.

**What writing it forced:** Faster for the common case, obvious for a first-time user, usable on a phone with one hand, and possible to scan without reading. Four real things, none of which I was willing to lose.

**What the design became:** The filtered list as the main route, with a text box for describing a job when the list does not have it. Mixed, which is what most of these comparisons end in.

**Wrong turn:** The wrong turn is recording the decision without what the rejected version did better, because you already believe the choice and the list is uncomfortable to write. An empty rejected column usually means the decision was made before the comparison.

**Trade-off:** A mixed design is two things to build and maintain rather than one, and the text box will be used by a small minority.

**Unknown:** Still unknown: whether anybody will use the text box at all. It is one line of the design that could be removed after a month of watching.


### Try a supplied example

Section: practice. Stable action: step-5-try.

Supplied output (hand-written for practice; no model produced it). Asked how members should find tools, it recommends: “Replace the filters with a chat box. Conversation is more natural for everyone, and 80% of users prefer chat.” Source to check, a fortnight of the library’s enquiry emails: most requests name a tool, a day and a branch; about one in ten describes an unusual job; no survey of preferences exists.

What should the decision record say about this recommendation?

- Keep filters as the main route, add a text field for unusual jobs, and drop the unsourced 80%.
- Follow it for members on phones, where a chat box avoids tapping through filters.
- Follow it, since a chat box can handle the common request and the unusual one alike.

<details>
<summary>After your attempt</summary>

Keep filters as the main route, add a text field for unusual jobs, and drop the unsourced 80%. — The enquiries say the common request is three known choices, which filters handle fastest, and that unusual jobs are real but rare. “More natural for everyone” and “80% prefer chat” have no source, so they do not enter the decision.

Follow it for members on phones, where a chat box avoids tapping through filters. — On a phone, composing a sentence usually costs more than tapping three filters, which is the opposite of the claim.

Follow it, since a chat box can handle the common request and the unusual one alike. — It can handle both, slowly for the common case, which is most of the volume; and the claims behind it are unsupported.

Now record your own decision in step 5, with what the rejected version did better.

</details>

### Your decision for the new case, and why

Section: practice. Stable action: write-transfer-decision.

Write your decision first, then the reason it fits this new case. Compare with the example answers only after writing.

**New case.** Made-up case: A swimming pool’s app lets people book a lane. A model-written proposal says to replace the timetable grid with a chat assistant “because conversation is more inclusive”. The pool’s notes say most bookings are the same weekly slot, many swimmers book on the poolside with wet hands, and a few ask unusual questions, such as whether a lane suits training with fins.

**Task:** Decide which interface should be the main route and what, if anything, a chat is kept for, and explain your reasoning for each kind of swimmer.

<details>
<summary>Compare after writing</summary>

- Weak: Accepts “more inclusive” or “more natural” without checking the notes, and makes chat the main route for everyone.
- Adequate: Keeps the grid, or a one-tap repeat booking, as the main route because most bookings are a repeated slot and typing with wet hands is hard; keeps a text box or chat for unusual questions such as fins.
- Strong: As adequate, and names what the grid cannot express, counts taps for the weekly booking, and notes that “inclusive” needs checking for swimmers less fluent in the app’s language, for whom typing can be harder.

</details>

```text
I am a complete beginner learning product design. Teach me through a short activity, not a long lecture.

Lesson: Conversational interfaces, and their alternatives
What I am trying to do: Design one task two ways — as a conversation and as a form or list — and write which is better for whom, with the reasons.

Key idea or terms:
Common case: What most people want most of the time. It is usually expressible in three or four choices.
Long tail: The unusual requests, each rare, together common. They are where free text earns its place.
Example prompt: The sample request shown beside the box. It is how people learn what is possible, and without it a text box is a guessing game.

Supplied practice material (fictional or labelled practice, not my research):

Conversation is more natural for everyone, and 80% of users prefer chat.” Source to check, a fortnight of the library’s enquiry emails: most requests name a tool, a day and a branch; about one in ten describes an unusual job; no survey of preferences exists.

Activity: Give me one hand-written model output from the supplied case. Ask me to find an unsupported claim, choose a source that could verify it and rewrite the claim with an honest boundary.

Ask one question at a time, at most three questions. Give a small hint only if I ask; leave the decisions and revision to me. Use only the anonymized material I paste. Label role-play as simulation. Never invent participants, quotes, research results or measured impact. Do not award a score or pass. If evidence is missing, say what is missing. Finish by asking me to revise one part and explain why.
When the activity is finished, tell me to return to the course answer called “The task, its common case, and an example of its long tail” and write my own decision. Do not write that answer for me.
```
